# KITABU QUEST Grade 10 English

## Title Page

KITABU QUEST Grade 10 English

Kenya CBC content draft for review

Mascot: fox rabbit

This content-first draft was regenerated from Kitabu curriculum data using a topic-by-topic book plan. It is designed to help learners read, practise, discuss, create, and revise with confidence.

Cover artwork is intentionally deferred until all content has passed review and is marked ready-for-cover.

## How To Use This Book

This book helps Grade 10 learners study English through clear lessons, worked examples, activities, practice, and revision.

Use the inquiry questions to think first. Read the explanation. Try the guided example. Complete the activity. Answer the practice questions. Correct your work and ask for support where needed.

Read, speak, listen, write, and edit in connected language tasks.

Every unit has a local example, a misconception to avoid, success criteria, and a check for understanding.

## Learning Skills In This Book

Each chapter builds knowledge, skills, values, creativity, communication, collaboration, critical thinking, digital literacy, and self-confidence.

Look for these page types: Learn, Example, Activity, Practice, Reflection, Home Link, and Review.

How to study well: read the goal first, underline new words, try the example before reading the answer, discuss with a partner, and correct your work after feedback. Do not skip the reflection questions. They help you notice what you understand and what needs more practice.

## stories, poems, and dialogues: Lesson Opener

Learning area: stories, poems, and dialogues

Start here: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to stories, poems, and dialogues.

Curriculum link: This lesson follows the Grade 10 curriculum sequence and focuses on stories, poems, and dialogues.

By the end of this lesson sequence, you should be able to:
- listen to varied audio-visual texts for enjoyment and general information
- analyse a wide range of audio-visual recordings to obtain key details (titles, characters, events, and places)
- retell events from recordings of stories, poems or dialogues for oral comprehension

Inquiry questions:

## stories, poems, and dialogues: Learn

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to stories, poems, and dialogues.

Important idea: stories, poems, and dialogues.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

## stories, poems, and dialogues: Worked Example

Listening model for stories, poems, and dialogues: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## you use appropriate words and phrases in hotel, dining: knowledge of polite words, expressions and euphemisms: Worked Example

Polite language model for you use appropriate words and phrases in hotel, dining: knowledge of polite words, expressions and euphemisms: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to you use appropriate words and phrases in hotel, dining: knowledge of polite words, expressions and euphemisms, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

## Reading 1.2.1 Reading Fluency: preview a text and make predictions about characters: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 1.2.1 Reading Fluency: preview a text and make predictions about characters: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes Common and proper Count and non-count Concrete and abstract nouns: classify nouns as proper: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Common and proper Count and non-count Concrete and abstract nouns: classify nouns as proper,: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

## Word Classes Common and proper Count and non-count Concrete and abstract nouns: phrasal quantifiers with count: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Common and proper Count and non-count Concrete and abstract nouns: phrasal quantifiers with count: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

## Writing 1.5.1 Sentence: sentences in which the comma has been used: Worked Example

Language model for Writing 1.5.1 Sentence: sentences in which the comma has been used: Use punctuation to guide meaning.

Original sentence: before the debate chairperson said we must listen carefully

Revision steps:
1. Begin with a capital letter.
2. Add a comma after the opening time phrase where it helps the reader pause.
3. Put spoken words inside quotation marks if the sentence reports exact speech.
4. Improved sentence: Before the debate, the chairperson said, "We must listen carefully."

Explanation: Capital letters, commas, and quotation marks help the reader know where the sentence begins, pauses, and reports speech.

## Writing 1.5.1 Sentence: well-written sentences in communication: Worked Example

Language model for Writing 1.5.1 Sentence: well-written sentences in communication: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

## Critical Listening speaker, context, audience, and intention selecting relevant key points avoiding: select key points: Worked Example

Listening model for Critical Listening speaker, context, audience, and intention selecting relevant key points avoiding: select key points: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Reading 2.2.1 Extensive: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 2.2.1 Extensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes: Verbs Main and Auxiliary Verbs, Tense and Aspect primary auxiliary: appropriate use of forms: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes: Verbs Main and Auxiliary Verbs, Tense and Aspect primary auxiliary: appropriate use of forms: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: verbs main and auxiliary verbs, tense and aspect primary auxiliary: appropriate use of forms yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: verbs main and auxiliary verbs, tense and aspect primary auxiliary: appropriate use of forms yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about word classes: verbs main and auxiliary verbs, tense and aspect primary auxiliary: appropriate use of forms: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Word Classes: Verbs Main and Auxiliary Verbs, Tense and Aspect primary auxiliary: form one particle phrasal: Worked Example

Media writing model for Word Classes: Verbs Main and Auxiliary Verbs, Tense and Aspect primary auxiliary: form one particle phrasal: Turn facts into a short, lively article opening.

Task: Write the opening of a school news article about a clean-water campaign.

Weak opening: The campaign was good and many people came.

Improved opening: Learners at Baraka Secondary turned the assembly ground into a clean-water action centre on Friday, testing simple filters, interviewing health volunteers, and signing a pledge to report broken taps before water is wasted.

Why it works:
1. It names who, where, and when.
2. It uses active verbs: turned, testing, interviewing, signing.
3. It gives concrete details instead of saying only good.
4. It fits a public audience.

Your turn: Write a 60-word blog or newspaper opening for word classes: verbs main and auxiliary verbs, tense and aspect primary auxiliary: form one particle phrasal using one fact, one vivid verb, and one clear audience.

## Writing 2.4.1 Mechanics: punctuation, abbreviations, and acronyms appropriately for effective communication: Worked Example

Language model for Writing 2.4.1 Mechanics: punctuation, abbreviations, and acronyms appropriately for effective communication: Use punctuation to guide meaning.

Original sentence: before the debate chairperson said we must listen carefully

Revision steps:
1. Begin with a capital letter.
2. Add a comma after the opening time phrase where it helps the reader pause.
3. Put spoken words inside quotation marks if the sentence reports exact speech.
4. Improved sentence: Before the debate, the chairperson said, "We must listen carefully."

Explanation: Capital letters, commas, and quotation marks help the reader know where the sentence begins, pauses, and reports speech.

Your turn: Write one sentence linked to writing 2.4.1 mechanics: punctuation, abbreviations, and acronyms appropriately for effective communication and punctuate it so a reader can follow it easily.

## Intensive Listening for specific details Note-taking Context: (narratives, factual texts): intensive listening for specific details note-taking: Worked Example

Listening model for Intensive Listening for specific details Note-taking Context: (narratives, factual texts): intensive listening for specific details note-taking: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## nonverbal cues enhance communication: humour, storytelling, music performance or art to convey: Worked Example

Reading model for nonverbal cues enhance communication: humour, storytelling, music performance or art to convey: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word classes: adjectives, simple prepositions, Proper adjectives - Kenyan, Japanese Regular: Worked Example

Language model for Word classes: adjectives, simple prepositions, Proper adjectives - Kenyan, Japanese Regular and: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: adjectives, simple prepositions, proper adjectives - kenyan, japanese regular and, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: addition, contrast, ordering, similarity, and summarising linking words: Worked Example

Writing model for Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: addition, contrast, ordering, similarity, and summarising linking words: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 3.4.1 paragraphing: addition, contrast, ordering, similarity, and summarising linking words by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 3.4.1 paragraphing: addition, contrast, ordering, similarity, and summarising linking words by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

## Selective Listening Instruction Directions Filtering information: filter required information from an oral text: Worked Example

Listening model for Selective Listening Instruction Directions Filtering information: filter required information from an oral text: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## one consider when giving or receiving feedback: give and receive feedback during a conversation: Worked Example

Oral language model for one consider when giving or receiving feedback: give and receive feedback during a conversation: Prepare a clear contribution for discussion.

Topic: How can learners communicate this idea clearly and respectfully?

Model response:
1. Position: I think one consider when giving or receiving feedback: give and receive feedback during a conversation needs clear examples and careful listening.
2. Reason: A listener understands better when the speaker gives one point at a time.
3. Evidence: In class, a speaker who names the topic, gives a reason, and pauses for questions is easier to follow.
4. Respectful close: I understand another group may explain it differently, but every answer should stay linked to evidence.

Speaking checklist: face the listener, speak at a steady pace, give one example, and respond politely to another person's point.

## Reading 4.2.1 Study: library etiquette when accessing and using library resources: Worked Example

Polite language model for Reading 4.2.1 Study: library etiquette when accessing and using library resources: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to reading 4.2.1 study: library etiquette when accessing and using library resources, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

## Reading 4.2.1 Study: print and digital library resources to research information: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 4.2.1 Study: print and digital library resources to research information: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes Determiners Demonstratives Possessives 4.3.2 Phrases Noun phrases Verb phrases: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Determiners Demonstratives Possessives 4.3.2 Phrases Noun phrases Verb phrases: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes determiners demonstratives possessives 4.3.2 phrases noun phrases verb phrases yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes determiners demonstratives possessives 4.3.2 phrases noun phrases verb phrases yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about word classes determiners demonstratives possessives 4.3.2 phrases noun phrases verb phrases: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Writing 4.4.1 Mechanics: advocate the correct use of punctuation marks: Worked Example

Language model for Writing 4.4.1 Mechanics: advocate the correct use of punctuation marks: Use punctuation to guide meaning.

Original sentence: before the debate chairperson said we must listen carefully

Revision steps:
1. Begin with a capital letter.
2. Add a comma after the opening time phrase where it helps the reader pause.
3. Put spoken words inside quotation marks if the sentence reports exact speech.
4. Improved sentence: Before the debate, the chairperson said, "We must listen carefully."

Explanation: Capital letters, commas, and quotation marks help the reader know where the sentence begins, pauses, and reports speech.

Your turn: Write one sentence linked to writing 4.4.1 mechanics: advocate the correct use of punctuation marks and punctuate it so a reader can follow it easily.

## Interactive Listening Negotiating meaning Turn taking Clarifying meaning: pick out instances of turn-taking and negotiating meaning: Worked Example

Listening model for Interactive Listening Negotiating meaning Turn taking Clarifying meaning: pick out instances of turn-taking and negotiating meaning: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Life Lessons in Plays: interrupt and disagree politely for peaceful co-existence: Worked Example

Polite language model for Life Lessons in Plays: interrupt and disagree politely for peaceful co-existence: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to life lessons in plays: interrupt and disagree politely for peaceful co-existence, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

## Reading 5.2.1 Intensive: predictions about events, people, and places: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 5.2.1 Intensive: predictions about events, people, and places: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes: Adverbs Gradable adverbs, of place, time, and manner: adverbs, adjectives, and prepositions in a sentence: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes: Adverbs Gradable adverbs, of place, time, and manner: adverbs, adjectives, and prepositions in a sentence: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: adverbs gradable adverbs, of place, time, and manner: adverbs, adjectives, and prepositions in a sentence yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: adverbs gradable adverbs, of place, time, and manner: adverbs, adjectives, and prepositions in a sentence yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

## Writing 5.4.1 Functional: Worked Example

Writing model for Writing 5.4.1 Functional: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 5.4.1 functional by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 5.4.1 functional by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

## Responsive Listening Connecting with a speaker Showing empathy Avoiding distractions: outline techniques of listening actively: Learn

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Responsive Listening Connecting with a speaker Showing empathy Avoiding distractions: outline techniques of listening actively.

Important idea: Responsive Listening Connecting with a speaker Showing empathy Avoiding distractions: outline techniques of listening actively.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

## Responsive Listening Connecting with a speaker Showing empathy Avoiding distractions: outline techniques of listening actively: Worked Example

Listening model for Responsive Listening Connecting with a speaker Showing empathy Avoiding distractions: outline techniques of listening actively: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Reading 6.2.1 Reading: features of expressive reading from a selected text: Worked Example

Media writing model for Reading 6.2.1 Reading: features of expressive reading from a selected text: Turn facts into a short, lively article opening.

Task: Write the opening of a school news article about a clean-water campaign.

Weak opening: The campaign was good and many people came.

Improved opening: Learners at Baraka Secondary turned the assembly ground into a clean-water action centre on Friday, testing simple filters, interviewing health volunteers, and signing a pledge to report broken taps before water is wasted.

Why it works:
1. It names who, where, and when.
2. It uses active verbs: turned, testing, interviewing, signing.
3. It gives concrete details instead of saying only good.
4. It fits a public audience.

Your turn: Write a 60-word blog or newspaper opening for reading 6.2.1 reading: features of expressive reading from a selected text using one fact, one vivid verb, and one clear audience.

## Reading 6.2.1 Reading: acknowledge the importance of reading fluency in communication.: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 6.2.1 Reading: acknowledge the importance of reading fluency in communication.: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes Relative pronouns: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Relative pronouns 6: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes relative pronouns 6, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Word Classes Relative pronouns: Learn

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Word Classes Relative pronouns 6.

Important idea: Word Classes Relative pronouns 6.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 10, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

## Word Classes Relative pronouns: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Relative pronouns 6: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes relative pronouns 6, then use two of them in correct sentences.

2. Write two correct sentences that use the grammar point, underline the key word or punctuation mark, and explain why it is correct. Target outcome: advocate the correct use of relative clauses.

## Writing 6.4.1 Creative: outline the structure of narrative and descriptive compositions: Worked Example

Writing model for Writing 6.4.1 Creative: outline the structure of narrative and descriptive compositions: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 6.4.1 creative: outline the structure of narrative and descriptive compositions by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 6.4.1 creative: outline the structure of narrative and descriptive compositions by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

## Writing 6.4.1 Creative: narrative paragraph to tell a story or recount: Worked Example

Reading model for Writing 6.4.1 Creative: narrative paragraph to tell a story or recount: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Critical Listening Distinguishing facts opinions: select specific details from an oral text: Worked Example

Listening model for Critical Listening Distinguishing facts opinions: select specific details from an oral text: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Speaking Fluency: Pronunciation and Enunciation Sounds /f/, /ŋ/ and consonant blend /: articulate the consonant sound: Worked Example

Pronunciation and vocabulary model for Speaking Fluency: Pronunciation and Enunciation Sounds /f/, /ŋ/ and consonant blend /: articulate the consonant sound: Hear, say, use, and check the word.

Word set: school, shade, share, shape.

1. Listen for the beginning sound.
2. Say each word clearly and slowly.
3. Use one word in a sentence: The learners sat under the shade during reading time.
4. Check meaning from the sentence, not from guessing.
5. Practise with a partner and correct one unclear sound.

2. Say each word clearly and slowly.
3. Use one word in a sentence: The learners sat under the shade during reading time.
4. Check meaning from the sentence, not from guessing.
5. Practise with a partner and correct one unclear sound.

## Reading 7.2.1 Intensive: predictions and evaluate events, characters, and places: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 7.2.1 Intensive: predictions and evaluate events, characters, and places: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Intensive Listening: visuals interpreting visuals Listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases: Learn

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Intensive Listening: visuals interpreting visuals Listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases,.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

## Intensive Listening: visuals interpreting visuals Listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases: Worked Example

Listening model for Intensive Listening: visuals interpreting visuals Listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases,: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Intensive Listening: visuals interpreting visuals Listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases: Activity And Practice

Activity for Intensive Listening: visuals interpreting visuals Listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases: Complete the listening, reading, grammar, writing, or speaking task named in the outcomes below, then improve one answer after feedback.

Use this routine for every answer: identify the exact language skill, state the audience or purpose, use evidence or an example, organise the response, and edit one sentence.

1. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases, and sentences.
2. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: make predictions about events or characters in an oral text.
3. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: answer direct and inferential questions for listening comprehension.

Correction check:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Reflection: What meaning became clearer after editing? Which sentence still needs work?

Home link: Ask someone at home for a short story, instruction, opinion, or example linked to intensive listening: visuals interpreting visuals listening with a purpose: pick out selected sounds, words, phrases. Write four clear sentences about it.

## Reading 8.2.1 Study Skills: SQ4R technique, summary, and note-making skills for study: Worked Example

Original note: A school debate helps learners practise listening, organise arguments, respond to different views, and speak with confidence before an audience.

Important idea: Clause Patterns SV (subject+ verb), SVO (subject+ verb+ object), SVOO (subject +: subject, verb, object, indirect.

## Clause Patterns SV (subject+ verb), SVO (subject+ verb+ object), SVOO (subject +: subject, verb, object, indirect: Worked Example

Language model for Clause Patterns SV (subject+ verb), SVO (subject+ verb+ object), SVOO (subject +: subject, verb, object, indirect: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss clause patterns sv (subject+ verb), svo (subject+ verb+ object), svoo (subject +: subject, verb, object, indirect yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed clause patterns sv (subject+ verb), svo (subject+ verb+ object), svoo (subject +: subject, verb, object, indirect yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about clause patterns sv (subject+ verb), svo (subject+ verb+ object), svoo (subject +: subject, verb, object, indirect: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Selective Listening: Filtering and Extracting Information from Oral Texts: strategies for listening to specific details: Worked Example

Listening model for Selective Listening: Filtering and Extracting Information from Oral Texts: strategies for listening to specific details: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 10 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

3. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: extract specific information such as characters, the message among others from an oral text.

## it important to accurately pronounce sounds: it important to accurately pronounce sounds in clear: Learn

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

## Reading 9.2.1 Critical/Close: short text multiple times to gain a deeper: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 9.2.1 Critical/Close: short text multiple times to gain a deeper: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 10 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Sentences Active voice Passive voice: Worked Example

Language model for Sentences Active voice Passive voice 9: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

Important idea: Writing 9.4.1 Functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 10, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

## Writing 9.4.1 Functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication: Worked Example

Language model for Writing 9.4.1 Functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

Your turn: Write one correct sentence about writing 9.4.1 functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication, then underline the subject and verb.

## Writing 9.4.1 Functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication: Activity And Practice

Activity for Writing 9.4.1 Functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication: Complete the listening, reading, grammar, writing, or speaking task named in the outcomes below, then improve one answer after feedback.

Use this routine for every answer: identify the exact language skill, state the audience or purpose, use evidence or an example, organise the response, and edit one sentence.

1. Write two correct sentences that use the grammar point, underline the key word or punctuation mark, and explain why it is correct. Target outcome: use the passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication clarity.
2. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: use writing 9.4.1 functional in clear listening, speaking, reading, writing, or grammar tasks.

Correction check:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Reflection: What meaning became clearer after editing? Which sentence still needs work?

Home link: Ask someone at home for a short story, instruction, opinion, or example linked to writing 9.4.1 functional: passive voice (sentences) in short reports for communication. Write four clear sentences about it.

## Answer And Teacher Notes

A strong English answer should:

- match the purpose and audience;
- use evidence from the passage, discussion, task, or real context;
- organise ideas into clear sentences and paragraphs;
- choose words that fit the tone;
- edit spelling, punctuation, grammar, and meaning after feedback.

For reading questions, use point, evidence, and explanation. For writing tasks, plan before writing and revise at least one sentence before submitting.

Answer-key guide: reading answers need a point, a quoted or paraphrased detail, and an explanation. Grammar answers need the corrected sentence and the reason for the correction. Writing answers need purpose, audience, structure, and editing evidence. Oral tasks need a clear position, reason, example, and respectful close.

Rubric for self-checking:
- 4: The answer addresses the exact question, uses subject vocabulary correctly, gives evidence or working, and includes a useful check or correction.
- 3: The main answer is correct but one part needs stronger evidence, clearer working, better labels, or a fuller explanation.
- 2: Some understanding is shown, but the answer is incomplete, too general, or hard to follow.
- 1: The answer is guessed, off topic, or missing the evidence needed for the task.

Correction routine: reread the command word, underline the key data or evidence, improve one weak step, and write one sentence explaining why the final answer is reasonable.

## Final Project

Create a final project for English.

Your project must include:
1. A clear title.
2. A real-life problem or question.
3. Evidence from at least three lessons.
4. A drawing, table, map, chart, model, dialogue, performance plan, artwork note, or worked solution.
5. A short reflection explaining what you learned.

Present your project to a classmate, teacher, parent, or study group.
